<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>YEAR ENDED DECEMBER 31, 2004</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Net Sales</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our net sales from continuing operations increased 13&nbsp;percent in 2004 to $3,763.3&nbsp;million from
$3,318.9&nbsp;million in 2003. Demand for our products remained robust, which was supported by stronger
economic conditions and continued low interest rates. In addition, we benefited from favorable
weather both at the start and end of the year. Excluding divested operations, all our product
lines recorded higher shipments for the full year compared to last year, with cement, aggregates
and gypsum setting new records for volumes. Compared to 2003, our selling prices generally
increased with the exception of our paving business. In addition, net sales benefited from
continued strength of the Canadian dollar, which finished the year close to 83 cents per U.S.
dollar compared to 78 cents per U.S. dollar in the prior year. Excluding the exchange rate effect,
net sales from continuing operations were 10&nbsp;percent higher than last year.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">18
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;U.S. net sales from continuing operations increased to $2,113.9&nbsp;million, up 12&nbsp;percent
compared to $1,882.0&nbsp;million in 2003, driven by high cement, aggregate and gypsum sales. Canadian
net sales increased 15&nbsp;percent to $1,649.4&nbsp;million in 2004 (7&nbsp;percent excluding the favorable
exchange rate effect) primarily due to higher cement, aggregate and paving sales.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Aggregates, Concrete &#038; Asphalt</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net sales from the aggregates, concrete and asphalt segment, formerly identified as
construction materials segment, totaled $2,274.1&nbsp;million in 2004, which were 12&nbsp;percent higher than
the $2,032.5&nbsp;million in 2003. Excluding the impact of the favorable Canadian exchange rate, net
sales were up 7&nbsp;percent with stronger sales across all product lines.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net sales of construction aggregates were $732.1&nbsp;million, which were 19&nbsp;percent higher than
2003 (15&nbsp;percent higher excluding the favorable exchange rate). Shipments of construction
aggregates increased 12&nbsp;percent in 2004 to a record 133.3&nbsp;million tons as a result of good weather
conditions and solid overall demand in the U.S. and in Canada. U.S. volumes were up 11&nbsp;percent as
a result of higher sales in the Great Lakes, Maryland and Missouri. Canadian volumes were 13
percent higher than 2003 levels due to strong sales in Western Canada, certain Ontario markets and
Montreal as well as increased exports to the U.S. west coast. Average selling prices in 2004 were
up 2&nbsp;percent in Canada, in local currency, and 3&nbsp;percent in the U.S. due in part to a better
product mix as well as successful implementation of general price increases in most markets.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ready-mixed concrete sales totaled $751.0&nbsp;million in 2004, up 9&nbsp;percent from last year (5
percent higher, excluding the favorable exchange rate). Overall ready-mixed concrete shipments
increased by 3&nbsp;percent to 11.4&nbsp;million cubic yards as stronger demand in western Canada offset a
flat U.S. market. Strong sales in Maryland, New York and New Mexico were offset by lower volumes
in Louisiana (completion of New Orleans airport project) and Missouri. Canadian volumes increased
by 6&nbsp;percent over 2003, as residential construction activity in Vancouver and Calgary remained
strong. However, price improvement gains in most regions were offset by pricing pressures in the
Denver, Toronto and Montreal markets.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Asphalt and paving sales totaled $692.3&nbsp;million in 2004, up 9&nbsp;percent from 2003 (up 3&nbsp;percent,
excluding the favorable exchange rate). Asphalt volumes were up 6&nbsp;percent compared to prior year
due to strong demand, particularly in Calgary and Denver, and more favorable weather. Excluding
divested operations, paving volumes were up 8&nbsp;percent due to strong demand in Calgary, Edmonton and
Denver.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Cement</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Cement net sales from continuing operations increased 13&nbsp;percent to $1,352.7&nbsp;million in 2004
from $1,198.3&nbsp;million in 2003 (up 10&nbsp;percent excluding the favorable exchange rate). Cement
shipments in 2004 reached a record 14.2&nbsp;million tons, which were 8&nbsp;percent higher than 2003
shipments excluding divested Florida operations. Strong general economic conditions and favorable
weather contributed to make 2004 an exceptionally strong volume year. Some regional cement
shortages were experienced, particularly in the first half of the year, requiring imports in order
to meet customer demand. Sales volumes in the U.S., excluding divested Florida operations,
increased by 8&nbsp;percent compared to 2003, driven by strong demand in all major markets except the
Great Lakes markets, which were flat. In Canada, sales volumes increased 6&nbsp;percent as demand was
up sharply in Western Canada.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Average selling prices continued their positive trend that began in the second quarter, driven
by successful price increases in a majority of our markets between January and May. For the first
time in more than a decade, we were able to successfully implement a second price increase in
selected U.S. markets allowing us to recover most of the shortfall due to the price degradation
that occurred in the second half of 2003. Average selling prices increased by 2&nbsp;percent
year-over-year, with the U.S. gaining 3&nbsp;percent and Canada increasing by 2&nbsp;percent in local
currency. We expect the full benefit of these price increases will be realized during 2005. In
addition, new price increases of $6 to $8 per ton took effect in most of our U.S. and Canadian
markets on January&nbsp;1, 2005, and an additional price increase of $5 per ton in most markets has been
announced in the U.S. effective July&nbsp;1, 2005.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">19
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Gypsum</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net sales from gypsum operations increased by 28&nbsp;percent to $326.6&nbsp;million from $254.3&nbsp;million
in 2003 benefiting from strong demand as well as the impact of four price increases implemented
throughout the year. Sales volume reached 2.2&nbsp;billion square feet, which represents an increase of
4&nbsp;percent over 2003 sales volume. We experienced continued growth of residential construction both
in the U.S. and Canada, supported by significantly improved manufacturing performance by our
plants. The average mill net price per thousand square feet for the year was $123 compared with
$98 in 2003, mainly derived from the strong performance of our U.S. markets. A price increase went
into effect on January&nbsp;10, 2005.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Performance by Line of Business</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Aggregates, Concrete &#038; Asphalt</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our aggregates, concrete and asphalt segment earned $207.8&nbsp;million on sales of $2.3&nbsp;billion,
13&nbsp;percent higher than 2003 earnings of $183.7&nbsp;million on sales of $2.0&nbsp;billion. Profit growth was
driven primarily by the aggregates business, which alone generated a $32&nbsp;million profit
improvement. The favorable impact of the Canadian dollar, which contributed $8.6&nbsp;million to
reported profit for the year, was more than offset by a $9.0&nbsp;million increase in pension and other
postretirement expenses. In addition, higher fuel costs across all product lines and increased
maintenance costs with higher plant activity resulted in increased operating costs. As a result,
segment operating income as a percentage of net sales at 9&nbsp;percent was flat compared to 2003.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Construction aggregates recorded an operating income of $155.8&nbsp;million in 2004, which was 26
percent higher than 2003, driven by record volumes and higher selling prices across all regions.
This was partially offset by cost increases, including additional quarry stripping costs of $5
million and higher energy costs of $4&nbsp;million. Operating margin improved by 1&nbsp;percentage point in
2004 compared to the year-ago level.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ready-mixed concrete profits declined by $3.9&nbsp;million to $30.8&nbsp;million in 2004. Most of the
shortfall came from the Denver, Toronto and Montreal markets where market conditions and
competitive pressures limited our ability to recoup increased raw material prices. The strong
performance of our Western Canada and Eastern U.S. markets partially offset the shortfall.
Performance based distribution initiatives continued to help offset increased delivery, maintenance
and fuel costs. Operating margin was 1&nbsp;percentage point lower than last year.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Asphalt and paving operations earned $31.0&nbsp;million in 2004, which was flat compared to 2003.
Paving contract margins declined, which more than offset benefits from increased asphalt volumes
and the absence of losses associated with the Western U.S. portable highway paving business we
exited in 2003. Increased use of recycled products helped offset higher costs during the year.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Cement</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Cement operating profit from continuing operations was $328.4&nbsp;million in 2004, which was 13
percent higher than last year. The results reflect increased volumes, higher prices and the
stronger Canadian dollar, which were partially offset by increased fuel, raw material and
distribution costs. Strong general economic conditions and sustained construction activity allowed
us to successfully implement two price increases in selected U.S. markets. A $12.1&nbsp;million
favorable impact of the Canadian dollar partially offset a $2.4&nbsp;million increase in pension and
other postretirement expenses and $10.6&nbsp;million of legal expenses related to settled litigation.
Clinker production at 12.8&nbsp;million tons was up 900,000 tons for the year. This 7.5&nbsp;percent
production increase was achieved without increasing plant fixed costs from 2003 levels. Several of
our plants achieved record production levels for the year. Variable costs were up for the year,
primarily driven by fuel and power costs increases which unfavorably impacted operating earnings by
$7.4&nbsp;million. Freight costs increased $2.0&nbsp;million due to sub-optimal shipping patterns we
experienced to serve our customers during the tight market conditions earlier this year.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As a result, cement operating income from continuing operations as a percentage of net sales
was 24.3&nbsp;percent, which was similar to 2003. In 2004, we increased our imports by approximately
200,000 short tons to meet our customer needs. The low-margin sales of imported cement combined
with the legal fees associated with the settled litigation negatively impacted our 2004 operating
margin.

</DIV>

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Gypsum</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our gypsum drywall operations recorded $33.3&nbsp;million operating profit in 2004 compared to a
loss of $5.0&nbsp;million in 2003, an improvement of $38.4&nbsp;million. Our gypsum operations have
generated profit for the past 6 quarters, as we benefited from continued strong residential demand
and higher prices. In addition, significantly improved manufacturing productivity improved our
overall performance, partially offsetting higher paper costs and increased energy expenses.
Production volumes from our two newer plants in Kentucky and Florida now account for 72&nbsp;percent of
total production.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Corporate and Unallocated Expenses</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Corporate and unallocated expenses in 2004 were $87.5&nbsp;million, which represents an increase of
$30&nbsp;million compared to 2003. The prior year included a $31.2&nbsp;million gain on the sale of our
Detroit cement terminal. Higher corporate expenses in 2004, due to increased pension and other
postretirement expenses, higher salaries, governance and compliance costs, were partially offset by
$10.6&nbsp;million of lower foreign exchange transaction losses.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Income from Managed Assets</B>




<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Income from the managed assets amounted to $16.3&nbsp;million in 2004, which represents an increase
of $4.3&nbsp;million compared to 2003 as a result of a management incentive fee we received from Lafarge
S.A. for exceeding agreed upon performance targets. Gross proceeds from the management of these
assets totaled $244.4&nbsp;million in 2004, as compared to $206.4&nbsp;million in 2003. Total proceeds
included billings for the annual management fee ($16.3&nbsp;million in 2004 and $12.0&nbsp;million in 2003),
as well as direct payroll, pension and postretirement costs and allocated selling, general and
administrative expenses. Expenses associated with management of these assets were $228&nbsp;million and
$194.4&nbsp;million in 2004 and 2003, respectively. We are fully reimbursed for these expenses under
the terms of the management agreement.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Interest Expense</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Interest expense for the year was $48.8&nbsp;million, $6.0&nbsp;million lower than last year. The fixed
to floating rate interest rate swap we executed in the first quarter of 2004 reduced interest
expenses by $4.9&nbsp;million. In addition, interest expense on our short-term debt borrowings
decreased by $1&nbsp;million versus 2003 due to lower average borrowing levels.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Interest Income</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Interest income for the year was $16.3&nbsp;million, which represents an increase of $15.2&nbsp;million
compared to 2003. The increase is due to the recognition of lower currency translation losses on
U.S. dollar investments in Canada, which favorably impacted the year-over-year comparison by $6.8
million. In addition, we recognized $4.9&nbsp;million of interest income associated with an income tax
receivable and interest income increased by $1.9&nbsp;million as a result of higher average investment
balances.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Income Taxes</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Income tax expense from continuing operations increased to $145.9&nbsp;million in 2004 from $133.2
million in 2003. Our tax expense was impacted in both 2003 and 2004 by certain unusual items. In
2004, we recorded a tax benefit of $6.3&nbsp;million. In 2003, we recognized an additional $7.6&nbsp;million
of tax expenses to reflect provincial tax changes legislated in both Ontario and Alberta, as well
as the higher tax rate applicable to the sale of the Detroit cement terminal in the third quarter.
Excluding these tax adjustments, the effective tax rate for 2004 and 2003 was approximately 35
percent and 36&nbsp;percent, respectively.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Net Income</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net income was $295.5&nbsp;million or $3.86 per diluted share, compared with net income in 2003 of
$280.6&nbsp;million, or $3.79 per diluted share. Current year results included several unusual items
that contributed a net benefit of $0.03 per diluted share. These unusual items included a tax
benefit of $6.3&nbsp;million ($0.08 per diluted share), interest income of $4.9&nbsp;million ($3.2&nbsp;million
after taxes or $0.04 per diluted share) associated with an income tax receivable partially offset
by legal expenses of $10.6&nbsp;million ($6.9&nbsp;million after taxes or $0.09 per diluted share) related to
settled litigation in our cement segment. Prior year results included income from discontinued
operations of $66.3&nbsp;million ($0.90 per diluted

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>YEAR ENDED DECEMBER 31, 2003</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Net Sales</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Lafarge&#146;s net sales from continuing operations increased 5&nbsp;percent in 2003 to $3,318.9&nbsp;million
from $3,160.1&nbsp;million in 2002. Demand for our products remained solid and we benefited during the
third and fourth quarters from the pent up demand that built up during the first half of the year,
when market conditions were particularly difficult due to poor weather. Cement, aggregate and
gypsum wallboard shipments for the full year were above last year, while shipments of ready-mixed
concrete were flat, and asphalt and paving sales fell short of last year. When compared to 2002,
our selling prices generally increased, with the exception of the U.S. cement market and our paving
business. In addition, net sales benefited from continued strength of the Canadian dollar, which
finished the year close to 78 cents per U.S. dollar. Excluding the exchange rate effect, net sales
from continuing operations were equal to last year.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Excluding the favorable exchange rate effect, the increase in cement and gypsum net sales was
partially offset by the drop in sales of our aggregates, concrete and asphalt segment. By region,
U.S. net sales from continuing operations decreased to $1,882.0&nbsp;million in 2003 compared to
$1,935.3&nbsp;million in 2002, driven by lower cement and asphalt and paving sales. Canadian net sales
increased 17&nbsp;percent to $1,436.9&nbsp;million in 2003 (5&nbsp;percent excluding the favorable exchange rate
effect), primarily due to higher cement, aggregate and ready-mixed concrete sales.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Aggregates, Concrete &#038; Asphalt</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net sales from the aggregates, concrete and asphalt segment totaled $2,032.5&nbsp;million in 2003,
5&nbsp;percent higher than the $1,943.0&nbsp;million in 2002. Excluding the impact of favorable Canadian
exchange rate, net sales were down 2&nbsp;percent. Stronger sales in construction aggregates were
offset by a decline in asphalt and paving sales.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net sales of construction aggregates were $614.5&nbsp;million, 15&nbsp;percent higher than 2002 (8
percent higher excluding the favorable exchange rate). Shipments of construction aggregates
increased 2&nbsp;percent in 2003 to 118.9&nbsp;million tons, with U.S. volumes up 7&nbsp;percent as a result of
higher sales in Missouri and New Mexico as well as higher resales in the Great Lakes. Canadian
volumes were 3&nbsp;percent lower than 2002 levels due to lower shipments in most of our Western
Canadian markets as well as lower exports to the U.S. west coast. Average selling prices in 2003
were up 4&nbsp;percent in Canada, in local currency, and 3&nbsp;percent in the U.S. due in part to a better
product mix as well as successful implementation of general price increases in most markets.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ready-mixed concrete sales totaled $689.6&nbsp;million in 2003, up 6&nbsp;percent from last year (flat
to 2002, excluding the favorable exchange rate). Overall ready-mixed concrete shipments fell
slightly to 11.0&nbsp;million cubic yards with stronger demand in Canada offsetting a 12&nbsp;percent volume
decline in Western U.S. markets. The deterioration in the construction economy in Colorado and New
Mexico resulted in both lower volume and prices. Canadian volumes increased by 5&nbsp;percent over
2002, with sustained project work in the energy/mining sector (Montreal and Vancouver). In
addition, average Canadian prices (in local currency) increased by 2&nbsp;percent compared to 2002 to
strong project pricing and higher value added sales.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Asphalt and paving sales totaled $637.8&nbsp;million in 2003, down 4&nbsp;percent from 2002 (down 11
percent, excluding the favorable exchange rate). Asphalt and paving volumes were down 6&nbsp;percent
and 14&nbsp;percent, respectively, in 2003 compared to prior year. Lower volumes in the U.S. were due
primarily to weather in the first half of the year, weak construction activity in our principal
Western U.S. markets as well as our exit from our Western U.S. portable highway paving business in
the second quarter of 2003. Further contributing to lower sales was lower demand in Eastern Canada
due to a weather related slow start of the paving season and less asphalt paving content in
projects.

</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->

<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Cement</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Cement net sales from continuing operations increased 6&nbsp;percent to $1,198.3&nbsp;million in 2003
from $1,127.2&nbsp;million in 2002 (up 3&nbsp;percent excluding the favorable exchange rate). With strong
shipments in the fourth quarter, full year sales volumes from our continuing operations in 2003
reached 13.2&nbsp;million tons, up 3&nbsp;percent from 2002. Sales volumes in the U.S. increased a modest 1
percent compared to 2002 while in Canada, sales volumes increased 7&nbsp;percent as demand was up
sharply in the western provinces. Average selling prices in Canada were up 3&nbsp;percent in local
currency, reflecting the impact of general price increases implemented early in the year. Prices
in the U.S. declined 1&nbsp;percent as the planned April price increase met with limited success and
some price degradation occurred due to competitive activity in several markets.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Gypsum</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net sales from gypsum operations increased by 9&nbsp;percent to $254.3&nbsp;million from $232.7&nbsp;million
in 2002, benefiting from strong demand as well as the impact of the price increases implemented in
the second half of the year. Sales volume reached 2.1&nbsp;billion square feet, an increase of 5
percent from 2002, which is in line with the increase in demand reported by the gypsum association
for the markets served by our plants. The average mill net price per thousand square feet for the
year was $98 compared with $96 in 2002. Prices increased significantly during the fourth quarter
2003 and a further price increase was implemented in January&nbsp;2004.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Performance by Line of Business</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Aggregates, Concrete &#038; Asphalt</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our aggregates, concrete and asphalt segment earned $183.7&nbsp;million on sales of $2.0&nbsp;billion,
down 7&nbsp;percent from 2002 earnings of $196.8&nbsp;million on sales of $1.9&nbsp;billion. The favorable impact
of the Canadian dollar, which contributed $10.6&nbsp;million to reported profit for the year, was more
than offset by an $18.7&nbsp;million dollar increase in pension and other postretirement costs. In
addition, losses incurred in our Western U.S. portable highway paving business that we exited in
the second quarter of 2003 and lower levels of paving activities in Colorado reduced profits for
the year. As a result, operating income as a percentage of net sales was 9&nbsp;percent in 2003
compared to 10&nbsp;percent in 2002.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Construction aggregates recorded an operating income of $123.9&nbsp;million in 2003, in line with
last year, as higher volumes and prices were offset by pension expenses, which increased by $5.9
million, and higher operating costs resulting in part from weather-related inefficiencies.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ready-mixed concrete profits declined $6.2&nbsp;million to $34.7&nbsp;million in 2003. Most of the
shortfall was in the Western U.S. as continued weak market conditions resulted in a 12&nbsp;percent drop
in volumes and competitive price pressures. In Canada, ready-mixed concrete profits were equal to
last year as a decline in Eastern Canada, due largely to difficult market conditions in Toronto,
was offset by improved profitability in our Western Canadian operations, due to higher volumes in
Vancouver and generally higher margins. Our ready-mixed concrete operations were negatively
impacted by an annual increase of $5.3&nbsp;million in pension expense in 2003.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Asphalt and paving operations earned $30.8&nbsp;million in 2003, about $7.4&nbsp;million less than in
prior year. Profits in Eastern Canada increased by 39&nbsp;percent over 2002 as lower costs and
improved margins offset lower volumes. Weak market conditions in the Western U.S., combined with
losses associated with the portable highway paving operations that we exited during the year,
resulted in a significant drop in earnings in that market, more than offsetting the improvement in
Eastern Canada. In addition, pension expense for this product line increased $5.3&nbsp;million in 2003.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Cement</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Cement operating profit from continuing operations was $290.5&nbsp;million, in line with last year.
The favorable impact of the Canadian dollar was $15.5&nbsp;million, which offset a $15.3&nbsp;million
increase in pension and other postretirement costs. Higher volumes and higher prices in Canada
were partially offset by weaker U.S. prices. In addition, margins declined as most of the
increased tonnage sold in the second half of the year came out of inventory rather than incremental
production. In terms of operating costs, fixed costs were lower by $3.8&nbsp;million due to lower
maintenance expenses as well as the absence of costs related to the start up of our plant near
Kansas City and the Bath strike in 2002. Variable


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">23
</DIV>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">costs per ton was down slightly for the year as significant improvement in fuel cost at our
Exshaw plant, due to the conversion to coal from natural gas, was largely offset by a reduction in
the availability and pricing of fuel quality waste used in some of our plants. Finally, the
profitability of our cement-related products business declined in 2003, primarily due to lower slag
results and clinker sales volumes. As a result, cement operating income from continuing operations
as a percentage of net sales was 24&nbsp;percent in 2003, which was down from 26&nbsp;percent in the prior
year.



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Gypsum</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our gypsum drywall operations returned to profitability starting the second half of 2003, as
we benefited from continued strong residential demand and higher prices, particularly in the fourth
quarter. For the full year, the gypsum operations lost $5.0&nbsp;million, which represented an
improvement of $11.3&nbsp;million from 2002 due to a combination of improved manufacturing performance
as well as price and volume increases. Production volumes from our two newer plants in Kentucky
and Florida accounted for 71&nbsp;percent of total production compared to 62&nbsp;percent last year. The
idling of our Wilmington plant in late 2002 resulted in the anticipated 2003 cost savings of
approximately $10&nbsp;million. However, higher natural gas prices, increased wage rates, higher labor
costs related to increased production levels in our remaining plants, as well as increased pension
costs and higher depreciation expense offset the Wilmington savings in 2003. The 2002 operating
loss of $16.3&nbsp;million excludes a $26&nbsp;million charge recorded in &#147;Other (income)&nbsp;expense, net&#148; for
the idling of our Wilmington, Delaware plant.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Corporate and Unallocated Expenses</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Corporate and unallocated expenses in 2003 were $57.5&nbsp;million, which represented a decrease of
$13.2&nbsp;million compared to 2002. The decrease was due to a $31.2&nbsp;million gain on the sale of the
Detroit cement terminal partially offset by a $10.1&nbsp;million increase in foreign exchange
transaction losses and the absence of an $8.1&nbsp;million credit recognized in 2002 related to a
natural gas derivative that did not qualify for hedge accounting. The provision of $26&nbsp;million
recognized in 2002 for the idling of our gypsum drywall plant in Wilmington was offset by a gain on
the sale of our Canadian paving stone business in that same year.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Income from Managed Assets</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Income from the managed assets amounted to $12&nbsp;million in both 2003 and 2002. Gross proceeds
from the management of these assets totaled $206.4&nbsp;million in 2003, compared to $190.8&nbsp;million in
2002. Total proceeds included billings for the annual management fee ($12.0&nbsp;million in both
years), as well as direct payroll, pension and postretirement costs and allocated selling, general
and administrative expenses. Expenses associated with management of these assets were $194.4
million and $178.8&nbsp;million in 2003 and 2002, respectively. We are fully reimbursed for these
expenses under the terms of the management agreement.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Interest Expense</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Interest expense for the year was $54.8&nbsp;million, up $2.4&nbsp;million from 2002 due to the absence
of $4.0&nbsp;million of capitalized interest in 2003 and a $1.2&nbsp;million interest expense increase from a
long-term bond issued in June&nbsp;2003. These were partially offset by a reduction in short-term
interest expense due to a combination of lower debt levels and lower interest rates.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Interest Income</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Interest income was $1.1&nbsp;million, a decrease of $8.6&nbsp;million from 2002 as foreign exchange
losses of $10.5&nbsp;million on U.S. dollar investments in Canada, due to the devaluation of the U.S.
dollar, offset an increase in interest from investments.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Income Taxes</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Income tax expense from continuing operations increased from $93.1&nbsp;million in 2002 to $133.2
million in 2003, including an $7.6&nbsp;million tax expense increase in 2003 due to provincial tax
changes legislated in both Ontario and Alberta as well as the higher tax rate applicable to the
sale of the Detroit cement terminal in the third quarter. The 2002 results benefited from the
recognition of approximately $28&nbsp;million in tax benefits related to the reversal of a deferred tax

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Net Income</B>

share), compared with $11.7&nbsp;million, or $0.16 per diluted share, in 2002.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>MANAGEMENT&#146;S DISCUSSION OF CASH FLOWS</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows summarize our main sources and uses of cash. These
statements show the relationship between the operations presented in the Consolidated Statements of
Income and liquidity and financial resources depicted in the Consolidated Balance Sheets.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our liquidity requirements arise primarily from the funding of capital expenditures, working
capital needs, debt service obligations and dividends. We usually meet our operating liquidity
needs through internal generation of cash except in the event of significant acquisitions.
Short-term borrowings are generally used to fund seasonal operating requirements, particularly in
the first two calendar quarters.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Cash Flows from Operations</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The net cash provided by operations for each of the three years presented reflects our net
income adjusted for the net effects of operating transactions and other events that affect net
income and operating cash flows in different periods. Significant adjustments are discussed below,
except for changes in working capital, which are discussed in Management&#146;s Discussion of Financial
Position.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The non-cash adjustment for depreciation, depletion and amortization in 2004 increased by
$18.5&nbsp;million reflecting new assets placed in service resulting from an increase in capital
expenditures ($176.4&nbsp;million) and the commencement of amortization of certain information
technology projects. The non-cash adjustment for depreciation, depletion and amortization in 2003
increased by $10.7&nbsp;million due to annualization effect of assets put in service in the course of
2002 (mainly the Sugar Creek cement plant and the Chicago slag operation), offset by the change in
useful lives of our cement plants in July&nbsp;2002 (6&nbsp;month impact of $8.5&nbsp;million decrease). The
non-cash adjustment for gain on sale of assets for 2003 and 2002 was relatively similar ($38.8
million in 2003 and $35.2&nbsp;million in 2002), as both years were impacted by large divestment gains
(Detroit cement terminal in 2003 and paving stone divestiture in Eastern Canada in 2002). The
impact of the gain on the sale of discontinued operations (Lafarge Florida Inc.), including related
income taxes paid, is shown separately. As a result of improving liquidity, we significantly
reduced the amount of securitized receivable financing. The impact of this change reduced the 2004
comparative cash flows from operations by approximately $87&nbsp;million. The adjustment for &#147;Net
change in noncurrent assets and liabilities&#148; primarily reflects the change in pension
contributions, which increased by $61.2&nbsp;million and $45.5&nbsp;million in 2004 and 2003, respectively.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">25
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Cash Flows from Investing</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2004, net cash used for investing activities increased $174.7&nbsp;million to $258.3&nbsp;million.
Capital expenditures more than doubled during 2004 as compared with 2003, from $173.9&nbsp;million to
$350.3&nbsp;million, due to $29.7&nbsp;million of additional capital expenditures associated with our Sugar
Creek, Missouri cement plant (see &#147;Note 20 Commitments and Contingencies&#148; to the consolidated
financial statements for more information), increased spending on information technology projects,
costs associated with the replacement terminal in Detroit, Michigan, and timing factors associated
with various projects. Redemption of short-term investments, net, for 2004 was $34.3&nbsp;million
compared to purchases of short-term investments, net in 2003 of $46.3&nbsp;million, both years
reflecting the change in the amount of excess cash in Canada that was invested in short-term
investments with original maturities exceeding three months. Proceeds from the sale of
non-strategic assets, surplus land and other miscellaneous items totaled $66.6&nbsp;million in 2004
compared to $71.2&nbsp;million in 2003. Proceeds for 2004 includes several individually insignificant
sale transactions while proceeds from 2003 include $38.2&nbsp;million from the sale of the Detroit
cement terminal.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2003, net cash used for investing decreased $107.7&nbsp;million compared to 2002. Capital
expenditures decreased $74.0&nbsp;million in 2003 largely because ongoing development projects, such as
the construction of the Sugar Creek, Missouri plant and Chicago slag project, were completed in
2002. Spending on acquisitions decreased $14.8&nbsp;million to $16.7&nbsp;million as only a few small
acquisitions were made. Purchases of short-term investments, net, for 2003 was $46.3&nbsp;million
compared to redemptions of short-term investments, net for 2002 of $1.0&nbsp;million, both years
reflecting the change in the amount of excess cash in Canada that was invested in short-term
investments with original maturities exceeding three months. Proceeds from the sale of
non-strategic assets, surplus land and other miscellaneous items totaled $71.2&nbsp;million in 2003
compared to $77.0&nbsp;million in 2002, as both years had significant proceeds related to the Detroit
cement terminal (in 2003) and paving stone divestiture in eastern Canada (in 2002) in addition to
the routine sales of surplus land and retail mobile equipment. The proceeds from the sale of
discontinued operations of $123.9&nbsp;million (Lafarge Florida Inc.), and related income taxes paid,
are shown separately.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Cash Flows from Financing</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2004, net cash provided by financing was $63.4&nbsp;million compared to net cash used by
financing activities of $126.2&nbsp;million in 2003. The change reflects an increase in short-term
borrowings in 2004 whereas we repaid short-term borrowings in 2003. Repayments were made in 2003
utilizing cash proceeds received in connection with the two large asset dispositions noted above.
Issuance of long-term debt for 2003 includes $47.0&nbsp;million of proceeds received in connection with
the issuance of industrial revenue bonds related to our Sugar Creek, Missouri plant. Also,
proceeds from issuance of equity securities increased to $52.3&nbsp;million in 2004 from $11.8&nbsp;million
in 2003 due to more employee stock option exercises in 2004.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2003, net cash consumed by financing was $126.2&nbsp;million compared to $85.8&nbsp;million in 2002.
This increase of $40.4&nbsp;million resulted from the net repayment of short-term borrowings of $131.1
million in 2003 versus net repayment of only $65.4&nbsp;million in 2002, an increase in dividends of
$6.4&nbsp;million and a decrease in the issuance of equity securities of $19.5&nbsp;million, offset by an
increase in long-term debt mainly due to the issuance of the industrial revenue bonds discussed
above.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We have access to a wide variety of short-term and long-term financing alternatives in both
the U.S. and Canada. These include a commercial paper facility under which we may from time to
time issue up to an aggregate principal amount of $300&nbsp;million in unsecured, short-term promissory
notes through private placements. The facility is 100&nbsp;percent backed by a syndicated, multi-year
committed bank revolver, which expires in April&nbsp;2007. Commercial paper borrowings as of December
31, 2004 and 2003 were $90&nbsp;million and zero, respectively. We also have a syndicated $300&nbsp;million
multi-year committed bank revolver maturing in April&nbsp;2007. At December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003, no
amounts were outstanding under the committed bank revolver.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We also have a receivables securitization program to provide a cost-effective source of
working capital and short-term financing. Under the program, we agreed to transfer, on a revolving
basis, an undivided percentage ownership interest in a designated pool of accounts receivable to
unrelated third-party purchasers up to a maximum of $200&nbsp;million. Under the agreements, new
receivables are added to the pool as collections reduce previously sold receivables. We service,
administer and collect the receivables sold, which as of December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003, were zero and


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">26
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">$82.0&nbsp;million, respectively. Since this program is accounted for as a sale of receivables,
the related cash flows are included in net cash provided by operations.



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2004, the strengthening of the Canadian dollar resulted in a positive effect of $50.7
million on the cash-flow statement, directly related to the impact of exchange rate changes on the
cash in Canada.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>MANAGEMENT&#146;S DISCUSSION OF FINANCIAL POSITION</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Consolidated Balance Sheets summarize our financial position at December&nbsp;31, 2004 and
2003.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We are exposed to foreign currency exchange rate risk inherent in our Canadian revenues,
expenses, assets and liabilities denominated in Canadian dollars, as well as interest rate risk
inherent in our debt. The value reported for Canadian dollar denominated net assets increased from
December&nbsp;31, 2003 as a result of a rise in the value of the Canadian dollar relative to the U.S.
dollar. At December&nbsp;31, 2004, the U.S. dollar equivalent of a Canadian dollar was $0.83 versus
$0.78 at December&nbsp;31, 2003. Based on 2004 results, if the value of the Canadian dollar relative to
the U.S. dollar changed by 10&nbsp;percent, our consolidated net assets and net income each would change
by approximately 6&nbsp;percent.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Working capital, excluding cash, short-term investments, short-term borrowings, redeemable
preferred stock, current portion of long-term debt and the impact of exchange rate changes ($50.8
million increase), increased $140.3&nbsp;million from December&nbsp;31, 2003 to December&nbsp;31, 2004.
Receivables, net (including &#147;Due from affiliates&#148;), excluding an exchange rate increase of $21.0
million, increased $166.2&nbsp;million primarily due to a decrease in receivables sold under our
securitization program and increased sales. Inventories decreased $28.3&nbsp;million, excluding the
exchange rate increase of $11.7&nbsp;million, due to increased levels of sales in the fourth quarter of
2004 compared to 2003. Accounts payable and accrued liabilities, excluding the exchange rate
increase of $14.4&nbsp;million, decreased $14.8&nbsp;million mainly due to a decrease in amounts payable
under our receivable sales agreement. Income taxes payable increased $17.7&nbsp;million due to stronger
earnings in the fourth quarter, which resulted in higher income taxes payable.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Net property, plant and equipment increased $77.8&nbsp;million during 2004, excluding the exchange
rate increase of $59.1&nbsp;million, as capital expenditures outpaced depreciation. No significant
acquisitions were made in 2004. Goodwill relates primarily to the Redland, Warren and Presque Isle
transactions.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our operating cash flows are generally sufficient to provide all of the cash operating
requirements, including capital expenditures, on an annual basis, although short-term debt to fund
seasonal working capital needs and operating losses is incurred during the first six months of the
year. We maintain sufficient liquidity resources, including a $300&nbsp;million commercial paper line,
a $200&nbsp;million accounts receivable securitization line, a committed $300&nbsp;million multi-year
revolver, a bi-lateral committed facility of $50&nbsp;million, and over $100&nbsp;million in uncommitted
bi-lateral bank facilities, to ensure that our cash requirements are met under any circumstances.
A $250&nbsp;million bond, issued in 1998 to fund an acquisition, will mature in July&nbsp;2005. At maturity,
we intend to use internal cash, short-term debt capacity, or a combination thereof, to repay the
bond.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As noted above, we decided that we will repatriate almost $1.1&nbsp;billion of Canadian earnings
under the repatriation provisions of the Jobs Act. Repatriation will substantially increase
liquidity in the United States, although use of the additional liquidity would be restricted by the
domestic reinvestment plan required by the Jobs Act. There will be a corresponding reduction in
liquidity at our Lafarge Canada Inc. (&#147;LCI&#148;) subsidiary. LCI may be required to borrow in order to
repatriate their earnings to the U.S. In addition, beginning on December&nbsp;29, 2005, Canadian $166.4
million (approximately U.S. $138.1&nbsp;million) in preferred shares issued by a Canadian subsidiary in
2000, in connection with our acquisition of the Warren Paving and Materials Group, will be
redeemable at the option of the holder. We also may be required to borrow if the holder of the
preferred shares elects to redeem these shares. We expect our significant positive Canadian cash
flows would be sufficient to repay any debt and replenish Canadian cash balances over time.

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The amounts set forth in the table above for noncapital purchase obligations relate primarily
to contractual purchase obligations related to gas, power, fuel and other significant raw material
commitments as well as mineral royalties. In addition, we are required to make contributions to
certain defined benefit plans. Our estimated 2005 contributions total $41.7&nbsp;million. Due to
uncertainties regarding significant assumptions involved in estimating future contributions, such
as interest rate levels and the amount and timing of asset returns, we are unable to reasonably
estimate our future contributions beyond 2005. We also have a number of contracts containing
commitments or contingent obligations that are not material to our earnings. These contracts are
discrete in nature, and it is unlikely that the various contingencies within the contract would be
triggered by a common event. The future payments under these contracts are not included in the
table set forth above.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We do not have any off-balance sheet arrangements that have, or are reasonably likely to have
a current or future material effect on our financial condition, liquidity or results of operations.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>MANAGEMENT&#146;S DISCUSSION OF SHAREHOLDERS&#146; EQUITY</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Consolidated Statements of Shareholders&#146; Equity summarize the activity in each component
of shareholders&#146; equity for the three years presented. In 2004, shareholders&#146; equity increased by
$453.2&nbsp;million, mainly from net income of $295.5&nbsp;million, a change in the foreign currency
translation adjustment of $124.5&nbsp;million (resulting from a 7&nbsp;percent increase in the value of the
Canadian dollar relative to the U.S. dollar), the reduction to accumulated other comprehensive loss
for $73.2&nbsp;million related to the minimum pension liability and the exercise of stock options
resulting in an increase of $47.2&nbsp;million. These were partially offset by share repurchases of
$49.1&nbsp;million and dividend payments, net of reinvestments, of $44.2&nbsp;million.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In 2003, shareholders&#146; equity increased by $500.0&nbsp;million, mainly from net income of $280.6
million, a change in the foreign currency translation adjustment of $276.8&nbsp;million (resulting from
a 22&nbsp;percent increase in the value of the Canadian dollar relative to the U.S. dollar), and the
exercise of stock options resulting in an increase of $7.7&nbsp;million. These were partially offset by
dividend payments, net of reinvestments, of $45.6&nbsp;million, and an increase to accumulated other
comprehensive loss for $36.9&nbsp;million related to the minimum pension liability.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Common equity interests include our $1.00 par value per share Common Stock and the Lafarge
Canada Inc. exchangeable preference shares (&#147;Exchangeable Shares&#148;), which are exchangeable into our
Common Stock and have comparable voting, dividend and liquidation rights. Our Common Stock is
traded on the New York and Toronto Stock Exchanges under the ticker symbol &#147;LAF&#148; and the
Exchangeable Shares on the Toronto Stock Exchange under the ticker symbol &#147;LCI.PR.E.&#148;


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">28

share per quarter.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>OTHER FACTORS AFFECTING THE COMPANY</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Critical Accounting Policies and Estimates</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Certain of our critical accounting policies require the use of judgment in their application
or require estimates of inherently uncertain matters. Although our accounting policies are in
compliance with generally accepted accounting principles, a change in the facts and circumstances
of the underlying transactions could significantly change the application of the accounting policy
and the resulting financial statement impact. Listed below are those policies that we believe are
critical and require the use of complex judgment in their application.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Goodwill </I>&#150; On January&nbsp;1, 2002, we adopted the provisions of SFAS No.&nbsp;142, &#147;Goodwill and Other
Intangible Assets.&#148; SFAS No.&nbsp;142 requires that goodwill be reviewed annually (or more frequently
if impairment indicators arise) for impairment. In our goodwill impairment test, we use a
combination of a market approach and an income approach when determining the fair value of our
reporting units. In the market approach, we compare our reporting units with similar businesses,
business ownership interests or manufacturing facilities that have been sold. In the income
approach, we estimate the discounted value of the expected future cash flows that we expect our
reporting units to generate based on our budgets and projections. Similar to our review for
impairment of long-lived assets, evaluations for impairment are significantly impacted by estimates
of future prices for our products, the evolution of expenses, economic trends in the construction
sector and other factors.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Pension Plans and Other Postretirement Benefits </I>&#150; The accounting for pension plans and other
postretirement benefits requires us to make certain assumptions that have a significant impact on
the expenses and liabilities that we record for pension plans and other postretirement benefits.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The primary assumptions made to account for pension plan and other postretirement benefit
expenses and liabilities are disclosed in &#147;Note 19 Pension Plan and Other Postretirement Benefits&#148;
of the consolidated financial statements.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">29
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The assumption for the expected long-term rate of investment return on pension plan assets is
based on historical performance, current and long-term outlook and the asset mix in the pension
trust funds. The discount rates reflect the rate of long-term high-grade corporate bonds.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The 2004 pension plan and other postretirement benefit expenses are impacted by the year-end
2003 assumptions for the discount rate and the return rate on assets (pension plan only). If the
2003 discount rate assumption had been lowered by one percentage point, 2004 pension plan and other
postretirement benefit expenses would have increased by approximately $18.5&nbsp;million. If the 2003
asset returns rate assumption had been lowered by one percentage point, 2004 pension plan expenses
would have increased by approximately $7.9&nbsp;million.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The pension and other postretirement accumulated benefit obligations are impacted by the 2004
discount rate. The impact of decreasing the discount rate assumption by one percentage point as of
November&nbsp;30, 2004 would have been to increase the total accumulated benefit obligation as of
December&nbsp;31, 2004 by approximately $148.0&nbsp;million, and would have increased the additional minimum
pension liability amount by approximately $219.4&nbsp;million.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Impairment or Disposal of Long-Lived Assets </I>&#150; We evaluate the recoverability of our long-lived
assets in accordance with the provisions of SFAS No.&nbsp;144, &#147;Accounting for the Impairment or
Disposal of Long-Lived Assets.&#148; SFAS No.&nbsp;144 requires that long-lived assets and certain
identifiable intangibles be reviewed for impairment whenever events or changes in circumstances
indicate that the carrying amount of an asset may not be recoverable. Recoverability of assets is
measured by comparing the carrying amount of an asset to future undiscounted net cash flows
expected to be generated by the asset. Such evaluations for impairment are significantly impacted
by estimates of future prices for our products, capital needs, economic trends in the construction
sector and other factors. If such assets are considered to be impaired, the impairment to be
recognized is measured by the amount by which the carrying amount of the assets exceeds their fair
value. Assets to be disposed of by sale are reflected at the lower of their carrying amount or
fair value less cost to sell.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We did not identify any impairment during 2004 and 2003. In November&nbsp;2002, an impairment
charge was recognized in relation to the idling of the gypsum drywall plant in Wilmington,
Delaware. This resulted in a $15&nbsp;million asset write-off.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Environmental Remediation Liabilities </I>&#150; When we determine that it is probable that a
reasonably estimable liability for environmental matters has been incurred, an undiscounted
estimate of the required remediation costs is recorded as a liability in the consolidated financial
statements, without offset of potential insurance recoveries. Environmental expenditures that
extend the life, increase the capacity or improve the safety or efficiency of company-owned assets
or are incurred to mitigate or prevent future environmental contamination are capitalized. Other
environmental costs are expensed when incurred.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Revenue Recognition </I>&#150; Revenue from the sale of cement, cement-related products, aggregates,
ready-mixed concrete, concrete products, asphalt and gypsum drywall is recorded when title and
ownership are transferred upon delivery of the products. Revenue from waste recovery and disposal
is recognized when the material is received, tested and accepted. Revenue from road construction
contracts is recognized on the basis of units of work completed, while revenue from other
indivisible lump sum contracts is recognized using the cost-to-cost method to determine the
percentage-of-completion. Amounts billed to a customer in a sales transaction related to shipping
and handling are included in &#147;Net Sales,&#148; and costs incurred by us for shipping and handling are
classified as &#147;Cost of goods sold.&#148;


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Inflation</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Inflation rates in recent years have not been a significant factor in our net sales or
earnings growth. We continually attempt to offset the effect of inflation by purchasing strategies
and improving operating efficiencies, especially in the areas of productivity, energy costs, and
selling and administrative expenses. We compete with other suppliers of our products in all of our
markets. The ability to recover increasing costs by obtaining higher prices for our products
varies with the level of activity in the construction industry, the number, size and strength of
competitors and the availability of products to supply a local market.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">30
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Environmental Matters</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our operations, like those of our competitors, are subject to federal, state, provincial and
local environmental laws and regulations that impose liability for cleanup or remediation of
environmental pollution and hazardous waste, require pollution control and prevention measures to
be undertaken and require certain permits and approvals for us to conduct many of our operations.
As a consequence, we maintain an environmental program designed to monitor and control
environmental matters relevant to us, including changes in applicable laws and regulations as well
as instances where corrective action may be needed. We recruit, employ and train personnel
experienced in environmental matters who are responsible for identifying developments relating to
environmental matters and bringing them to management&#146;s attention, including emergency reporting
and quarterly reporting of environmental matters. We routinely audit our properties to determine
whether environmental remediation may be required, the amount of any necessary related provisions,
the status of remedial activities undertaken and whether improvements may be required to meet
current and future environmental requirements.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We may capitalize environmental expenditures that extend the life, increase the capacity,
improve the safety or efficiency of assets or that are incurred to mitigate or prevent future
environmental contamination. We expense other environmental costs when incurred. Our
environmental capital expenditures and expenses were not material for the years ended December&nbsp;31,
2004, 2003 and 2002 and we do not believe that the ultimate resolution of our known environmental
contingencies, for which we have recorded provisions for estimated probable liabilities, will have
a material adverse effect on our financial condition, results of operations or liquidity. However,
our environmental expenditures have increased over time and are likely to increase in the future.
Changes to existing or new environmental laws or regulations, or interpretations thereof, may
impose substantial further environmental obligations on us. Such changes may include: revised EPA
Boiler and Industrial Furnaces regulations; final residual risk or maximum achievable control
technology provisions governing air toxic emissions; cement kiln dust management standards or
programs; revised state implementation plans regarding NO<SUB>x</SUB> reductions; revised state
implementation plans regarding reduction of fine particulate matter particles; federal &#147;major
source&#148; permit requirements; and implementation of the Kyoto Protocol. We cannot presently
determine the effect such changes may have on us, including the nature and amount of any required
capital expenditures or other remedial actions, or on our financial condition, results of
operations or liquidity.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>Item&nbsp;7A. QUANTITATIVE AND QUALITATIVE DISCLOSURES ABOUT MARKET RISK</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We are exposed to certain commodity price, interest rate and foreign currency risks. We have
defined strict policies and procedures to measure, manage and monitor our market risk exposures.
Our policies do not permit any speculative market position.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We use financial instruments only to hedge existing or anticipated financial and commercial
exposures. We undertake this hedging in the over-the-counter market with a limited number of
highly rated counter parties. Our positions in derivative financial instruments are monitored
using various techniques, including the fair value approach.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Commodity Price Risk</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We
are subject to commodity price risk with respect to price changes principally in coal,
petroleum coke, natural gas, electricity, diesel fuel and liquid asphalt cement. We attempt to
limit our exposure to changes in commodity prices by entering into long-term contracts, increasing
our use of alternative fuels, or using commodity-based derivatives. We execute commodity-based
derivatives only for the purpose of hedging the economic exposure to commodity price changes, not
for trading or speculative purposes.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;By using derivative financial instruments to hedge exposures to changes in commodity prices,
we are exposed to credit risk and market risk. Credit risk is the failure of the counter party to
perform under the terms of the derivative contract. When the fair value of a derivative contract
is positive, the counter party owes us, which creates credit risk for us. When the fair value of a
derivative contract is negative, we owe the counter party and, therefore, do not have a credit
risk. We minimize the credit risk in derivative instruments by entering into transactions only
with what we believe are low credit risk counter parties.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">31
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Market risk is the adverse effect on the value of a financial instrument that results from a
change in commodity prices. We manage market risk by establishing and monitoring parameters that
limit the types and degree of market risk that may be undertaken. As such, a 10&nbsp;percent change in
the commodity prices for hedged items would cause a change on our December&nbsp;31, 2004 consolidated
net assets and the 2004 consolidated other comprehensive income by less than $1.6&nbsp;million, net of
any other impact. Such a change would have no impact on net income as our derivatives qualify as
cash flow hedges with the effective portion of the fair value change reported as a component of
&#147;Accumulated Other Comprehensive Income/(Loss).&#148;


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Foreign Currency Risk</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Translation Risk </I>&#150; The assets, liabilities, income and expenses of our Canadian operating
entities are denominated in a foreign currency. Our financial statements are presented in U.S.
dollars. Thus, assets, liabilities, income and expenses denominated in currencies other than U.S.
dollars must be translated into U.S. dollars at the applicable exchange rate to be included in our
financial statements.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If the U.S. dollar increases in value against the Canadian dollar, the value in U.S. dollars
of assets, liabilities, income and expenses originally recorded in the Canadian dollar will
decrease. Conversely, if the U.S. dollar decreases in value against the Canadian dollar, the value
in U.S. dollars of assets, liabilities, income and expenses originally recorded in Canadian dollars
will increase. Thus, increases and decreases in the value of the U.S. dollar relative to the
Canadian dollar has a direct impact on the value in U.S. dollars of our Canadian dollar-denominated
assets, liabilities, income and expenses, even if the value of these items has not changed in their
original currency.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At December&nbsp;31, 2004 and for the year then ended, approximately 63&nbsp;percent of our consolidated
net assets and 60&nbsp;percent of consolidated net income was attributable to subsidiaries that prepare
their financial statements in Canadian dollars. As such, a 10&nbsp;percent change in the U.S.
dollar/Canadian dollar exchange rate would cause a change on consolidated net assets and
consolidated net income by approximately $195&nbsp;million and $18&nbsp;million, net of any other impact,
respectively.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Transaction Risk </I>&#150; We are subject to limited foreign currency exchange risks as a consequence
of commercial exposures related to purchase and sale transactions in currencies other than our
operating currencies. Our exposure to changes in foreign currency exchange risks related to
transactions is not material.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp; <I>Interest Rate Risk</I>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We are exposed to interest rate risk through our debt. We assess interest rate cash flow risk
by continually identifying and monitoring changes in interest rate exposures that may adversely
impact expected future cash flows and by evaluating hedging opportunities.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In February&nbsp;2004, we entered into a $200&nbsp;million interest rate swap which effectively converts
our $200&nbsp;million bond, maturing in July&nbsp;2013, from a fixed rate coupon of 6.9&nbsp;percent to a floating
rate coupon at six month LIBOR plus a fixed spread of 2.43&nbsp;percent. At December&nbsp;31 2004 we had no
other derivative instruments related to interest rates.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our interest rate exposure can be subdivided into the following risks:


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Price risk for fixed-rate financial assets and liabilities </I>&#150; By contracting a fixed-rate
liability, for example, we are exposed to an opportunity cost in the event of a fall in interest
rates. Changes in interest rates impact the market value of fixed-rate assets and liabilities,
leaving the associated financial income or expense unchanged. At December&nbsp;31, 2004 and after
giving effect to the interest rate swap noted above, 61&nbsp;percent of our consolidated indebtedness
was contracted at fixed rates.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>Cash-flow risk for floating rate assets and liabilities </I>&#150; Changes in interest rates have
little impact on the market value of floating-rate assets and liabilities, but directly influence
our future income or expense flows.


<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt">32
</DIV>

<!-- PAGEBREAK -->
<P><HR noshade><P>
<H5 align="left" style="page-break-before:always">&nbsp;</H5><P>

<DIV style="font-family: 'Times New Roman',Times,serif">

<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<I>We use variable-rate debt to finance our operations</I>. At December&nbsp;31, 2004, approximately 39
percent of our consolidated indebtedness was at variable rates, which exposes us to variability in
interest payments due to changes in interest rates. As such, a one percent change in the average
interest rate would have caused a change in 2004 consolidated net income by approximately $2.1
million, net of any other impact.

<P align="center" style="font-size: 10pt"><B>REPORT OF INDEPENDENT REGISTERED PUBLIC ACCOUNTING FIRM</B>



<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">The Board of Directors and Shareholders<BR>
Lafarge North America Inc.:


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We have audited the accompanying consolidated balance sheets of Lafarge North America Inc. (a
Maryland corporation) and subsidiaries as of December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003, and the related
consolidated statements of income, shareholders&#146; equity, comprehensive income and cash flows for
each of the three years in the period ended December&nbsp;31, 2004. Our audits also included the
financial statement schedule listed in the Index at Item&nbsp;15(a). These financial statements and
financial statement schedule are the responsibility of the Company&#146;s management. Our responsibility
is to express an opinion on these financial statements and financial statement schedule based on
our audits.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We conducted our audits in accordance with the standards of the Public Company Accounting
Oversight Board (United States). Those standards require that we plan and perform the audit to
obtain reasonable assurance about whether the financial statements are free of material
misstatement. An audit includes examining, on a test basis, evidence supporting the amounts and
disclosures in the financial statements. An audit also includes assessing the accounting principles
used and significant estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall financial
statement presentation. We believe that our audits provide a reasonable basis for our opinion


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In our opinion, the financial statements referred to above present fairly, in all material
respects, the consolidated financial position of Lafarge North America Inc. and subsidiaries at
December&nbsp;31, 2004 and 2003, and the consolidated results of their operations and their cash flows
for each of the three years in the period ended December&nbsp;31, 2004, in conformity with U.S.
generally accepted accounting principles. Also, in our opinion, the related financial statement
schedule, when considered in relation to the basic financial statements taken as a whole, presents
fairly in all material respects the information set forth therein.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As discussed in Note 1 to the consolidated financial statements, in 2003 the Company changed
its method of accounting for asset retirement obligations. Also, as discussed in Note 19 to the
consolidated financial statements, the Company changed the date used to measure pension and other
postretirement benefit plan assets and obligations.


<P align="left" style="font-size: 10pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We also have audited, in accordance with the standards of the Public Company Accounting
Oversight Board (United States), the effectiveness of Lafarge North America Inc.&#146;s internal control
over financial reporting as of December&nbsp;31, 2004, based on criteria established in Internal Control
&#150; Integrated Framework issued by the Committee of Sponsoring Organizations of the Treadway
Commission and our report dated February&nbsp;4, 2005 expressed an unqualified opinion thereon.
